        &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}}(\cos (\pi k) + i \sin (\pi k))  e^{ \pi i  \tau (k+k^2) } = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (-1)^k e^{ \pi i  \tau ((k+ \frac{1}{2})^2 - \frac{1}{4} )} \\
        &= e^{-\frac{\pi i  \tau}{4}}\sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (-1)^k e^{ \pi i  \tau (k+ \frac{1}{2})^2  } 
    \end{align*}
    We can see that 
    \[
    (k+\frac{1}{2})^2 = k^2 + k + \frac{1}{4} = 1 + 2k + k^2 -k -1 + \frac{1}{4} = (k+1)^2 - (k+1) + \frac{1}{4} = (- (k+1) + \frac{1}{2})^2.
    \]
    Using this and the fact that $|\mathbb{N}_0| = |\mathbb{Z}|$, it follows 
    \begin{align*}
    e^{-\frac{\pi i  \tau}{4}}\sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} (-1)^k e^{ \pi i  \tau (k+ \frac{1}{2})^2  } &= e^{-\frac{\pi i  \tau}{4}}\sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}_0} \left( (-1)^k e^{ \pi i  \tau (k+ \frac{1}{2})^2  } + (-1)^{-(k+1)} e^{ \pi i  \tau (-(k+1)+ \frac{1}{2})^2  } \right)  \\
    &= e^{-\frac{\pi i  \tau}{4}}\sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}_0} (1-1)^k e^{ \pi i  \tau (-(k+1)+ \frac{1}{2})^2  } =0.
    \end{align*}
}

\section{Heat Kernel of $(0, 1)$}
Here we want to construct a basis for $L^2((0,1))$, i.e. we need some eigenfunctions of the Laplace operator, that vanish on $0$ and $1$, i.e. the boundary, so that the boundary conditions are satisfied. The Eigenfunction of $- \frac{d^2}{dx^2}$ on $(0,1)$ that satisfies the Dirichlet fboundary conditions, i.e. having their roots at $\partial (0,1) = \{0,1\}$, are given by $\sqrt{2} \sin (k \pi x), k \in \mathbb{N}$: 
\[
- \frac{d^2}{dx^2} \sqrt{2} \sin (k \pi x)= - \sqrt{2} \frac{d}{dx} \cos (k \pi x) k \pi = \sqrt{2} k^2 \pi^2 \sin (k \pi x), \text{ i.e. $k^2 \pi^2$ are Eigenvalues.}
\]
Due to $k, k^\prime \in \mathbb{N}$:
\[
\int_0^1 \sqrt{2} \sin (k \pi x) \sqrt{2} \sin (k^\prime \pi x) dx \stackrel{???}{=} \int_0^1 \cos( (k-k^\prime) \pi x ) - \cos ((k+k^\prime ) \pi x) dx \stackrel{???}{=} \delta_{k,k^\prime}
\]
$(\sqrt{2} \sin (k \pi x))_{k \in \mathbb{N}}$ builds an orthonormal system of $C(0,1)$. Note that $(0,1) \cong \mathbb{R} / 2\mathbb{Z}$. Let $f$ be continuous on $[0,1]$ with roots at $\partial (0,1)$. Define the the odd reflection 
\[
\hat{f} (x) := \begin{cases}
    f(x) &, x \in (0,1) \\
    -f(-x) &, x \in (-1,0)
\end{cases}
\]
then $\hat{f}$ is continous on $(-1,1)$ and $\hat{f}(-1) = \hat{f}(0)=\hat{f}(1)=0$. If we repeat this pattern for $(1,2), (2,3),...$ we get 
\[
\tilde{f} (x) := \begin{cases}
    f(x-2n) &, x \in [2n,2n+1], n \in \mathbb{Z}\\
    -f(2n-x)&, x \in [2n-1,2n], n \in \mathbb{Z}
\end{cases}
\]
is continuous on $\mathbb{R}$ and has roots on $\mathbb{Z}$ and period $2$.
\begin{align*}
    \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} c_k e^{i \pi k x} &= \tilde{f}(x) \\
    &= - \tilde{f}(-x) = - \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} c_k e^{i \pi k (-x)} =- \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} c_{-k} e^{i \pi k x}  
\end{align*}
which implies that $\forall k \in \mathbb{Z}: c_k = - c_{-k}$. This yields 
\begin{align*}
    \tilde{f}(x) &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} \left( c_k e^{i \pi k x} + c_{-k} e^{- i \pi k x} \right) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} c_k \left( e^{i \pi k x} - e^{- i \pi kx}\right) \\
    &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} c_k 2i \sin (\pi k x) =: \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} b_k \sin (\pi k x).
\end{align*}
Thus, the ONB $(\sin (\pi k x))_{k \in \mathbb{N}}$ lies dense in the set 
\[
\mathcal{A} := \left\{ \tilde{f} \in L^2 (\mathbb{R} / 2\mathbb{Z}) \mid \tilde{f} (x)= \begin{cases}
 f(x-2n) &, x \in [2n,2n+1], n \in \mathbb{Z}\\
    -f(2n-x)&, x \in [2n-1,2n], n \in \mathbb{Z}
\end{cases}  \right\}.
\]
Due to the fact that 
\[
L^2((0,1)) \to \mathcal{A}, f \mapsto \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \tilde{f}
\]
(we need to scale with $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$ in order to get an isomporphism) satisfies 
\[
\langle \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\tilde{f},\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\tilde{g} \rangle_{\mathcal{A}} = \frac{1}{2} \int_0^2 \tilde{f}(x) \tilde{g}(x) dx =\frac{1}{2} 2 \int_0^1 f(x) g(x) dx =  \langle f,g  \rangle_{L^2((0,1))},
\]
i.e. this mapping is an isomporhism. This finally implies that $(\sqrt{2}\sin (\pi k x))_{k \in \mathbb{N}}$ builds an ONB of $L^2((0,1))$. Putting it all together, we can now write $\forall h \in L^2((0,1))$: 
\[
h(x) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} \underbrace{ \langle \sqrt{2} \sin (k \pi y) , h \rangle_{L^2} }_{=: a_k} \sqrt{2} \sin (k \pi x).
\]
For the initial value problem of the Heat equation 
\begin{align*}
    & \dot{u}+ Hu =0, \quad \text{ on } (0,1) \times \mathbb{R}^+ \\
    & u(x,0) = h(x), \quad x \in (0,1) \\
    & u(0,t)=u(1,t) =0, \quad t \in \mathbb{R}^+
\end{align*}
where $H:= - \frac{d^2}{dx^2}$, the solution is given by 
\begin{align*}
    \int_{(0,1)} H_{(0,1)} (x,y,t) h(y) dy &= u(x,t) = \left( e^{-tH} h \right)(x) \\
    &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} \langle \sqrt{2} \sin ( \pi y, h) \rangle_{L^2} e^{-t k^2 \pi^2} \sqrt{2} \sin (k\pi x) \\
    &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} 2 \int_0^1 \sin (k \pi y) h(y) dy e^{-t k^2 \pi^2} \sin (k \pi x) \\
    &= \int_0^1 \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} 2  \sin (k \pi y)   e^{-t k^2 \pi^2} \sin (k \pi x) h(y) dy
\end{align*}
which implies that 
\[
H_{(0,1)} (x,y,t) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} 2  \sin (k \pi y)    \sin (k \pi x) e^{-t k^2 \pi^2}.
\]
Using that $\sin (A) \sin (B) = \cos (A-B) - \cos (A+B)$ it follows that 
\[
H_{(0,1)} (x,y,t) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} e^{- \pi^2 k^2 t} \cos (k \pi (x-y)) - \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} e^{- \pi^2 k^2 t} \cos (k \pi (x+y)).
\]
Remembering Jacobi's Theta function: 
\begin{align*}
    \Theta (x,\tau ) &:= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} e^{\pi i k^2 /tau + 2 \pi i k x} = e^0 + \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} e^{\pi i k^2 \tau } \left( e^{ 2 \pi i k x} + e^{ -2 \pi i k x} \right) \\
    &= 1+ 2 \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} e^{\pi i k^2 \tau} \cos (2 \pi k x) \\
    &\iff \frac{1}{2} \Theta (x,\tau) - \frac{1}{2} = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} e^{\pi i k^2 \tau} \cos (2 \pi k x),
\end{align*}
leads to 
\begin{align*}
    H_{(0,1)} (x,y,t) &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} e^{- \pi^2 k^2 t} \cos (k \pi (x-y)) - \sum_{k \in \mathbb{N}} e^{- \pi^2 k^2 t} \cos (k \pi (x+y)) \\
    &= \frac{1}{2} \Theta (\frac{x-y}{2}, i t \pi) - \frac{1}{2} \Theta (\frac{x+y}{2}, i t \pi ).
\end{align*}
Interpretation: ???\\
\rmkb{
    Exercises:...
}
The heat kernel on the cartesian product of two domains can be easily be calculated as the heat kernels of both domains:
\lem{}{
    If $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^m$ and $\Omega^\prime \subset \mathbb{R}^n$ are two open, bounded and connected domains with heat kernels $H_\Omega$ and $H_{\Omega^\prime}$ respectively, the heat kernel of $\Omega \times \Omega^\prime$ is given  
    \[
    \forall (x,x^\prime),(y,y^\prime ) \in \bar{\Omega}\times \bar{\Omega}^\prime, t \in \mathbb{R}^+: \quad H_{\Omega \times \Omega^\prime} ((x,x^\prime),(y,y^\prime),t) = H_\Omega (x,y,t) H_{\Omega^\prime} (x^\prime,y^\prime ,t), 
    \]
}
\pf{
    We need to show that $H_{\Omega \times \Omega^\prime}$ satisfies the definition of the heat kernel of $\Omega \times \Omega^\prime$: 
    \begin{itemize}
        \item [i)] $\forall (x,x^\prime,t) \in \Omega \times \Omega^\prime \times \mathbb{R}^+: \quad (y,y^\prime) \mapsto H_{\Omega \times \Omega^\prime} ((x,x^\prime),(y,y^\prime),t)$ extends continuously to $\bar{\Omega}$ with $0$ on $\partial (\Omega \times \Omega^\prime)$.
        \item [ii)] For $(x,x^\prime) \in \Omega \times \Omega^\prime$ the function $(y,y^\prime,t) \mapsto H_{\Omega \times \Omega^\prime} ((x,x^\prime),(y,y^\prime),t) - \Phi (x-y,t) \Phi (x^\prime-y^\prime ,t)$ solves the homogenuous heat equation and extends continuously to $\bar{\Omega} \times \bar{\Omega}^\prime \times  \mathbb{R}_0^+$ with $0$ on $\bar{\Omega} \times \bar{\Omega}^\prime \times \{0\}.$
    \end{itemize}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item [i)] Due to $\partial (\Omega \times \Omega^\prime) = (\partial \Omega \times \bar{\Omega}^\prime ) \cup (\bar{\Omega} \times \partial \Omega^\prime)$ we have the two cases for $(\xi, \eta ) \in \partial (\Omega \times \Omega^\prime)$:
        \begin{itemize}
            \item Case 1: $(\xi, \eta ) \in  \bar{\Omega} \times \partial \Omega^\prime$: 
            \[
            \lim_{y^\prime \to \eta} H_\Omega (x,y,t) H_{\Omega^\prime} (x^\prime,y^\prime,t) = \lim_{y^\prime \to \eta}  H_\Omega (x,y,t) H_{\Omega^\prime}(x^\prime,y^\prime,t)=0
            \]
            \item Case 2:  $(\xi, \eta ) \in \partial \Omega \times \bar{\Omega}^\prime $: 
            \[
            \lim_{y \to \xi} H_\Omega (x,y,t) H_{\Omega^\prime} (x^\prime,y^\prime,t) = \lim_{y \to \xi}  H_\Omega (x,y,t) H_{\Omega^\prime}(x^\prime,y^\prime,t)=0
            \]
        \end{itemize}
        The continuous extension is trivial due to the product.
        \item  [ii)] First, note that 
         \[
        \Delta_{\Omega \times \Omega^\prime} = \sum_{j=1}^{n+m} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_j^2} = \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_i^2} + \sum_{k=1}^m \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_k^2} = \Delta_\Omega + \Delta_{\Omega^\prime} 
        \]
        leads to 
        \begin{align*}
            &(\frac{\partial }{\partial t} - \Delta_{\Omega \times \Omega^\prime} ) ( H_\Omega H_{\Omega^\prime} - \Phi^\prime \Phi )= (\frac{\partial }{\partial t} - \Delta_{\Omega} -  \Delta_{\Omega^\prime} ) ( H_\Omega H_{\Omega^\prime} - \Phi^\prime \Phi )\\
            &=\dot{H}_{\Omega} H_{\Omega^\prime} + H_{\Omega} \dot{H}_{\Omega^\prime} - \dot{\Phi} \Phi^\prime - \Phi \dot{\Phi}^\prime - \big( \Delta_\Omega H_\Omega H_{\Omega^\prime} + \Delta_{\Omega^\prime} H_{\Omega^\prime} H_\Omega - \Delta_\Omega \Phi \Phi^\prime - \Delta_{\Omega^\prime } \Phi^\prime \Phi   \big) \\
            &= (\dot{H}_\Omega - \Delta_\Omega H_\Omega) H_{\Omega^\prime} + (\dot{H}_{\Omega^\prime} - \Delta_{\Omega^\prime} H_{\Omega^\prime} ) H_\Omega - \big( (\dot{\Phi} - \Delta_\Omega \Phi ) \Phi^\prime + (\dot{ \Phi}^\prime - \Delta_{\Omega^\prime} \Phi^\prime) \Phi \big)=0
        \end{align*}
        where $\Phi^\prime $ is defined on $\Omega^\prime$ and $\Phi$ on $\Omega$.
    \end{itemize}
    Fix $x \in \Omega$. Define $W(x,y,t) :=  H_\Omega (x,y,t)- \Phi (x-y,t)$ then $(\partial_t - \Delta) W(x,y,t)=0$ on $\Omega \times (0,T]$. By definition $W(x,y,t) =0$ on $\bar{\Omega} \times \{0\}$. It holds that with using the first condition of the heat kernel that for $y \in \partial \Omega$ it holds $H_\Omega (x,y,t)=0$ which implies that 
    \[
    \forall y \in \partial \Omega: \quad W(x,y,t)= -\Phi (x-y,t)\leq 0.
    \]
    For $t\searrow 0$ both summands are the same delta distribution, i.e. $\lim_{t \searrow 0} W(x,y,0)=0$ for $x,y \in \bar{\Omega}$. Because we can write the boundary parabolic cylinder as $\partial \Omega_T = (\partial \Omega \times [0,T]) \cup (\bar{\Omega} \times \{0\})$ we have just shown that $W(x,y,t) \leq 0$ on $\partial \Omega_T$. Using the maximum principle it follows that $\forall (y,t) \in \Omega_T: W(x,y,t) \leq 0$. This implies 
    \[
    \forall (y,t) \in \Omega_T:\quad  H_\Omega (x,y,t) \leq \Phi (x-y,t) = \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{n/2}} \underbrace{e^{- \frac{|x-y|^2}{4t}}}_{\leq 1} \leq \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{n/2}}.
    \] 
    Thus, $H_\Omega$ is polynomially bounded $\mathcal{O} (t^{-n/2})$ for $t \to 0$.\\
    Define $d_x := dist (x, \partial \Omega)>0$. Then, for $t \in (0,T]$:
    \begin{align*}
        &\sup_{y \in \partial \Omega} |W(x,y,t)| = \sup_{y \in \partial \Omega} \left| \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{n/2}} e^{- \frac{|x-y|^2}{4t}} \right| \\
        &\leq \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{n/2}} e^{- \frac{d_x^2}{4t}}.
    \end{align*}
    Because with the maximum principle the solution is in the interior smaller than on the boundary, it follows 
    \[
    \forall (y,t) \in \Omega_T: \quad |W(x,y,t)| \leq \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{n/2}} e^{- \frac{d_x^2}{4t}}.
    \]
    Thus, as $t \to 0$ it follows that $H_\Omega (x,y,t) - \Phi (x-y,t)$ vanishes exponentially fast. \\
    Using this we finally have  
        \begin{align*}
           & \lim_{t \searrow 0} (H_{\Omega} (x,y,t) H_{\Omega^\prime} (x^\prime,y^\prime,t) - \Phi (x-y,t) \Phi^\prime (x^\prime-y^\prime,t) ) \\
           &= \lim_{t \searrow 0} \underbrace{ \left( H_{\Omega} (x,y,t)- \Phi (x-y,t) \right)}_{\in \mathcal{O}(e^{-\frac{1}{t}})} \underbrace{ H_{\Omega^\prime} (x^\prime,y^\prime,t)}_{\in \mathcal{O}(t^{-n/2})} + \underbrace{\Phi (x-y,t)}_{\in \mathcal{O}(t^{-n/2})} \underbrace{( H_{\Omega^\prime} (x^\prime,y^\prime,t) -  \Phi^\prime (x^\prime-y^\prime,t))}_{\in \mathcal{O}(e^{-\frac{1}{t}})}  =0.
        \end{align*}
}


We now have a formula for heat kernels on all tori $(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^n$ and on all cartesian products of $(0,1)^n$. However, the boundary $\partial (0,1)^n$ is not a continuously differentiable submanifold of $\mathbb{R}^n$. The faces are $n-1$ dimensional submanifolds, the edges and corners are $n-2$ dimionsal and $n-3$ dimensional submanifolds respectively. Thus, when applying the divergence theorem, we get additional boundary terms. Luckily, these additional terms vanish, due to the fact that they are zero sets on the $\mathbb{R}^{n-1}$ Lesbesgue measure. Thus, the divergence theorem still holds and we can use all previous results. Hence, for the initial value Problem
\[
\dot{ u} - \Delta u=0, \quad \text{ on } (0,1)^n \times (0,T], \quad u(x,0)=h(x), \quad x \in (0,1)^n, \quad u(x,t) =0, \quad x \in \partial (0,1)^n, t \in (0,T],
\]
the heat kernel is given by 
\[
H_{(0,1^n)}(x,y,t) = \prod_{i=1}^n H_{(0,1)} (x_i,y_i,t),
\]
thus for $y:= (y_1,...,y_n) \in (0,1)^n$ and $x=(x_1,...,x_n) \in (0,1)^n$ the solution is given by  
\begin{align*}
    u(x,t) = \int_{(0,1)^n} H_{(0,1)^n} (x,y,t) h(y) d^n y &= \int_{(0,1)^n} \prod_{i=1}^n H_{(0,1)} (x_i,y_i,t) h(y) d^n y .
\end{align*}
Due to 
\[
\Phi (x-y,t) = \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{n/2}} e^{- \frac{|x-y|^2}{4t}} = \frac{1}{r^n} \frac{1}{(4 \pi \frac{t}{r^2})^{n/2}} e^{- \frac{|\frac{x-y}{r}|^2}{4\frac{t}{r^2}}} = \frac{1}{r^n} \Phi \left( \frac{x-y}{r}, \frac{t}{r^2} \right) 
\]
it follows 
\begin{align*}
    H_{(0,r)} (x,y,t) &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \left( \Phi (x-y +2 kr,t) - \Phi (x+y+2kr,t) \right) \\
    &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \frac{1}{r} \left( \Phi (\frac{x-y +2 kr}{r},t/r^2) - \Phi (\frac{x+y+2kr}{r},t/r^2) \right) \\
    &= \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \frac{1}{r} \left( \Phi (\frac{x-y }{r} +2k,t/r^2) - \Phi (\frac{x+y}{r}+2k,t/r^2) \right) \\
    &= \frac{1}{r} H_{(0,1)} \left( \frac{x}{r}, \frac{y}{r}, \frac{t}{r^2} \right).
\end{align*}
This leads to 
\[
 H_{(0,r)^n} (x,y,t) = \prod_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{r} H_{(0,1)} \left( \frac{x_i}{r}, \frac{y_i}{r}, \frac{t}{r^2} \right) = \frac{1}{r^n} H_{(0,1)^n} \left( \frac{x}{r}, \frac{y}{r}, \frac{t}{r^2} \right).
\]
\cor{
    Any $u$ solution of the homogeneous heat equation on $[0,r]^n \times [0,T] \subset \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}$ satisfies 
    \[
    u(x,T) = - \underbrace{\int_0^T \int_{\partial [0,r]^n} u(z,t) \nabla_z H_{[0,r]^n} (x,z,T-t) \cdot N(z) d \sigma (z) dt}_{=0} + \int_{[0,r]^n}  \underbrace{u(y,0)}_{=h(y)} H_{[0,r]^n} (x,y,T) d^n y.
    \]
}
\cor{
    Any solution $u$ of the homogenuous heat equation on an open domain $\Omega_T \subset \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}$ is smooth and for fixed $t$ it is analyitic wrt. $x$.
}
\pf{
    \begin{itemize}
        \item To show $u \in C^\infty (\Omega \times (0,T])$ .\\
        Because $\Phi$ is smooth and $y \mapsto H_\Omega (x,y,t) - \Phi (x-y,t)$ extends continuously to $\bar{\Omega } \times \mathbb{R}^+$ it follows that $H_\Omega$ is smooth wrt. $x$ and $y$ (symmetry of heat kernel) on $\Omega \times \Omega \times \mathbb{R}^+$. Thus, $u$ from the corollary above is in $C^\infty (\Omega \times (0,T])$, because the integrant is smooth wrt. $x$ and $y$ and the integration is over $y$.
        \item To show that $u(\cdot , t)$ is analyitic for fixed $t$.\\
        For $y \in \partial \Omega$ it follows that $\Phi (x-y,t) \to 0$, $t \searrow 0$ and all partial derivatives of $\Phi$ also satisfy this property, i.e. $D^\alpha \Phi (x-y,t) \to 0$ for $t \searrow 0$. $H_\Omega$ inherits all of these property by definition of the heat kernel ii). Thus,
        \[
        \left| \frac{H_\Omega^{(n)} (x,\xi,t)}{n!} (x-y)^n \right| \to 0, \quad \forall \xi \in (x,y), \quad t \searrow 0.
        \]
        Thus, for fixed $t$ the Taylor series of $H_\Omega$ around $x$ converges $\forall y \in \Omega$. Because the integration and differentiation can be interchanged (due to smoothness of $H_\Omega$ wrt. $x$ and $y$) it follows that the Taylor series of $u(\cdot , t)$ around $x$ converges $\forall x \in \Omega$. Thus, $u(\cdot , t)$ is analyitic.
    \end{itemize}
    ???
}




\section{Summary}
We first noticed that scaling invariance of the soltuion to the heat equation on $\mathbb{R}^n$, where $u(x,t) $ is a solution $\iff$ $u(\lambda x,\lambda^2 t)$ is also a solution. This suggests the self-similar Ansatz $u(x,t) = v(\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}})$ leading to the fundamental solution $\Phi$. This fundamental solution is non negative, its integral is one, i.e. it is similar to mollifiers. I.e. we get something like an approximate identity if we take the convolution of $\Phi$ with the initial value as $t \searrow 0$. Also the solution to the homogeneuous initial value problem on $\mathbb{R}^n$ is the convolution $u=\Phi * h$ for $t>0$. As the solution depends on the entire $\mathbb{R}^n$ the speed of information propagation is infinite. Next, we showed that we can view the inhomogeneous heat equation as an ODE on an infinite dimensional functional space and showed that the solution is given by 
\[
\int_0^t \Phi (\cdot , t-s) * f(\cdot ,s ) ds.
\]
Further, the inhomogeneous heat equation can be obtained from choosing the initial value as $f(\cdot,t)$ and integrating over the solution of the homogeneous initial value problem. The solution to the Cauchy problem is then obtained by splitting the solution $u=v+w$ into the inhomogeneous and homogeneous initial value problem (superposition).\\
Unlike the Laplace equatin the solution to the heat equation satisfies a mean value property on a space time region (not a ball) called heat balls $E(x,t,r)$, which are defined through $\Phi$.\\
The maximums princpiles are defined on $\Omega_T$, where the parabolic boundary is $\partial \Omega_T = (\partial \Omega \times (0,T]) \cup (\Omega \times \{0\})$. If $u\in C^2$ is a solution of the heat equation on $\Omega_T$, which is path connected, and extends continuously to $\overline{\Omega_T}$ and takes its maximum in $\Omega_T$, then it is constant on $\Omega_T$. \\
If $\Omega$ is open and boundad and $u \in C^2$ is the solution to the heat equation extending constinuously to $\bar{\Omega_T}$, it follows that the maximum is on $\partial \Omega_T$. Using these we can show the existence and uniqueness of the Cauchy problem on an open and bounded domain.\\
On $\mathbb{R}^n$ the uniqueness is more constrained. A counterexample shows this. To show the uniqueness here, we first define the maximums principle for the Cauchy problem on $\mathbb{R}^n$ for bounded solutions 
\[
u(x,t) \leq A e^{a|x|^2}.
\]
This is then used to show the existence and uniqueness of such solutions on $\mathbb{R}^n$.\\
Another approach to show uniquness is the usage of energy functions 
\[
e(t) := \int_\Omega u^2 (x,t) d^n x.
\]
Up to now we have only found explicit forms for the solutions on the interior of the domain, by ignoring the boundary. Here heat kernels come into play. They are symmetric, positive and give us a form for the solution on $\bar{\Omega}$. Using spectral thoery we can show the existence of these heat kernels by deriving explicit formulas on $S^1$ and $(0,1)$ and finally generalizing to any $[0,r]^n \times [0,T]$. Further, this section shows that convolution with $\Phi$ is analougously to summation of eigenfunctions and gives us a justification to treat the heat equation as an ODE.

\chapter{Wave Equation}

We consider the homogeneous and inhomogenuous wave equation $\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u=0$ and $\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u=f$ respectively on open subsets of $\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}$. It is a linear second order PDE. The matrix for the coefficients of the differential operator has one positive and $n$ negative eigenvalues, i.e. it is neither definite or semi definite. The theory to derive a solution in this case is completely different to the chapters before. We have information that propagates with finite speed through space. See electromagnetic waves.
\section{D'Alemberts Formula $n=1$}
Consider the initial value proplem 
\begin{align*}
    &\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} u=0, \quad (x,t) \in \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^+,\\
    & u(x,0)=g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) = h(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}.
\end{align*}
We chose these initial conditions, because we interpret this PDE as an ODE on the vector space of smooth functions on $\mathbb{R}$, i.e. $C^\infty (\mathbb{R})$, then ODE theory suggests to fix an initial value and an initial velocity (first derivative) at an initial time $t_0$. We set $t_0=0$. We clearly want to have uniqueness of the solution.\\
Note that for $n=1$ we have 
\[
\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2}  - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} = \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial }{\partial x} \right) \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial }{\partial x} \right).
\]
In the homogenuous case we know that if $u$ solves the homogeneous wave equation, then $v(x,t) := \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial }{\partial x} \right) u(x,t)$ solves the transport equation $\frac{\partial}{\partial t} v + \frac{\partial }{\partial x} v=0$. Using the method of characteristics, the unique solution of this transport equation with initial value $v(x,0) = a(x)$ is given by
\[v(x,t) = a(x-t).
\]
Thus, we have
\[\left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial }{\partial x} \right) u(x,t) = a(x-t).
\]
This is an inhomogeneous transport equation with constant coefficients. Because $f^\prime (u(x,s))= -u(x,s)$ and choosing $x_0 =x + t$ the characteristic $X(s) := x+t-s$ and the solution $U(s) := u(X(s),s)$ along it then yield
\[
\frac{d}{ds} U(X(s),s) = \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u (X(s),s) +  \frac{\partial}{\partial x} u (X(s),s) \underbrace{\frac{dX}{ds}}_{=-1} = \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u (X(s),s) - \frac{\partial}{\partial x} u (X(s),s) = a(X(s)-s) = a(x+t-2s)
\]
is an ODE, whose solution is given by 
\[
U(t) - U(0) = \int_0^t a(x+t-2s) ds.
\]
Thus, we have for $b(x+t) := u(\underbrace{x+t}_{=X(0)},0)$ that 
\[
u(X(t),t) = \int_0^t a(x+t-2s) ds + b (x+t) \stackrel{y= x+t-2s, \quad dy=(-2) ds}{=} \int_{x+t-2\cdot 0}^{x+t-2t} (- \frac{1}{2}) a(y) dy + b(x+t) =  \frac{1}{2} \int_{x-t}^{x+t} a(y) dy + b(x+t).
\]
Inserting the initial values, i.e. 
\[
u(x,0) = b(x) = g(x) , \quad a(x) =v(x,0) = \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \right) u(x,0) = h(x) - g^\prime (x),
\]
we get 
\begin{align*}
    u(x,t) &= \frac{1}{2} \int_{x-t}^{x+t} h(y) - g^\prime (y) dy + g(x+t)\\
    &= - \frac{1}{2} (g(x+t) - g(x-t))  + g(x+t) + \frac{1}{2} \int_{x-t}^{x+t} h(y) dy \\
    &= \frac{1}{2} (g(x+t) + g(x-t)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_{x-t}^{x+t} h(y) dy.
\end{align*}
Thus we have used the homoegenous and the inhomogenous transport equation to derive 
\thm{D'Alemberts Formula}{
If $g: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ is twice continuously differentiable and $h: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ is continuously differentiable, then 
\[
u(x,t) =\frac{1}{2} (g(x+t) + g(x-t)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_{x-t}^{x+t} h(y) dy
\]
is twice continuously differentiable and the unique solution of the initial value problem
\begin{align*}
    &\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} u=0, \quad (x,t) \in \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^+,\\
    & u(x,0)=g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) = h(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}.
\end{align*}
}
Thus, we can write 
\[
u(x,t) = \frac{1}{2} (g(x+t) + g(x-t)) + \frac{1}{2} (H(x+t) -H(x-t)) = \underbrace{\frac{1}{2} (g(x+t) + H(x+t))}_{=: F(x+t)} + \underbrace{\frac{1}{2} (g(x-t) - H(x-t)) }_{=: G(x-t)}.
\]
It follows 
\[
 \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \right) F(x+t) = F^\prime (x+t) \cdot 1 - F^\prime (x+t) \cdot 1 =0,
\]
and 
\[
 \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \right) G(x-t) = G^\prime (x-t) \cdot (-1) + G^\prime (x+t) \cdot 1 =0,
\]
which implies 
\[
 \left( \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} \right) u(x,t) = \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \right) \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \right) (F(x+t) + G(x-t)) =0,
\]
if $F$ and $G$ are twice differentiable. We have shown that every function of the form $u(x,t) = F(x+t) + G(x-t)$ solves the homogeneous wave equation. \\

We interpret the fact, that the value of the solution at $(x,t)$ depends only on the values of $F$ and $G$ at $x+t$ and $x-t$ respectively, i.e. $u(x,t)$ depends excusively on the intervall $[x-t,x+t]$, which means information speed spreads with speed $1$ in both directions.\\

Further, in the heat equation we had the property that the solution increases in regularity over time. Here, the regularity stays the same, i.e. if $H$ and $G$ are k-times differentiable, then $u$ is also k-times differentiable.\\

In order to define the wave equation on $[0,\infty)$ the following approach will be of importance. Consider the initial value problem 
\begin{align*}
   & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u(x,t) - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} u(x,t) =0, \quad (x,t) \in \mathbb{R}^+ \times \mathbb{R}^+ \\
    & u(x,0) = g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) = h(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^+ , \quad u(0,t)=0, t \in \mathbb{R}_0^+. 
\end{align*}
Define the point reflection of $u,g,h$ as 
\[
\tilde{u}(x,t)= \begin{cases}
    u(x,t) &, x \geq 0 \\
    -u(-x,t) &, x\leq 0
\end{cases}, \quad \tilde{g}(x)= \begin{cases}
    g(x) &, x \geq 0 \\
    -g(-x) &, x\leq 0
\end{cases}, \quad \tilde{h}(x)= \begin{cases}
    h(x) &, x \geq 0 \\
    -h(-x) &, x\leq 0
\end{cases}
\]
then $\tilde{u}, \tilde{g},\tilde{h}$ are defined on the entire space $\mathbb{R}^+ \times \mathbb{R}_0^+$. Next, we see that for the solution $\tilde{u}$ of the initial value problem 
\begin{align*}
    & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{u}(x,t) - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2} \tilde{u}(x,t) =0, \quad (x,t) \in \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^+ \\
     & \tilde{u}(x,0) = \tilde{g}(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \tilde{u}(x,0) = \tilde{h}(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}
\end{align*}
the function $(x,t) \mapsto -\tilde{u}(-x,t)$ also solves the same initial value problem. By uniqueness (under sufficient regularity) of the solution it follows that both solution coincide: $\tilde{u} (x,t)= - \tilde{u}(-x,t)$.\\
\begin{itemize}
    \item For $t \leq x$ the solution $u(x,t)$ depends on the domain $[x-t,x+t] \subset \mathbb{R}^+_0$, i.e. only the non negative part of the real axis. Using D'Alemberts formula it follows 
    \[
    u(x,t)= \frac{1}{2} (g(x+t) + g(x-t)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_{x-t}^{x+t} h(y) dy.
    \]
    \item However, for $x \leq t$ the solution depends on the domain $[x-t,x+t]$ which contains negative values. Using the point reflection we get
    \begin{align*}
        \tilde{u}(x,t) &= \frac{1}{2} ( \underbrace{\tilde{g}(x+t)}_{=g(x+t)} + \underbrace{\tilde{g}(x-t)}_{=-g(t-x)}) + \frac{1}{2} \int_{x-t}^{x+t} \tilde{h}(y) dy \\
        &= \frac{1}{2} ( g(x+t) - g(t-x)) + \frac{1}{2} \left( \int_{x-t}^{t-x} \tilde{h}(y) dy+ \int_{t-x}^{x+t} \tilde{h}(y) dy  \right) \\
        &= \frac{1}{2} ( g(x+t) - g(t-x)) + \frac{1}{2}  \int_{t-x}^{x+t} h(y) dy 
    \end{align*}
    Although we used in the last step that an integral over an odd function on a symmetric intervall vanishes.
\end{itemize}
Thus, it total 
\[
u(x,t) = \begin{cases}
    \frac{1}{2} (g(x+t) + g(x-t)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_{x-t}^{x+t} h(y) dy &, 0\leq t \leq x \\
    \frac{1}{2} ( g(x+t) - g(t-x)) + \frac{1}{2}  \int_{t-x}^{x+t} h(y) dy &,0 \leq x \leq t
\end{cases}.
\]
This describes waves moving on $[0,\infty)$ where waves moving towards $x=0$ get reflected. This is prescribed using point reflection, i.e. the waves moves through $x=0$ and gets inverted.

\section{Spherical Means of the Wave equation}
The methods of spherical means is a technique that transforms the initial value problem of the wave equation on $\mathbb{R}^n$ into a lower dimensional problem by using the translation invariance and local rotaional averaging. Define $x \in \mathbb{R}^n, t \geq 0, r>0$: 
\[
U(x,r,t) := \frac{1}{n \omega_n r^{n-1}} \int_{\partial B_r (x)} u(y,t) d \sigma (y) =: \fint_{\partial B_r (x)} u(y,t) d \sigma (y),
\]
where $\fint$ denotes the average value and $\omega_n$ the volume of the n-dimensional unit ball. Analogously we define 
\[
G(x,r) := \fint_{\partial B_r (x)} g(y) d\sigma (y), \quad H(x,r) := \fint_{\partial B_r (x)} h(y) d\sigma (y).
\]
\lem{}{
    If $u\in C^m (\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+_0)$ is a m-times continuously differentiable solution of the initial value problem 
    \[
    \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u=0 , \quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+ , \quad u(x,0)=g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) = h(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n,
    \]
    then the spherical mean $ (r,t) \mapsto U(x,r,t)$ for fixed $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ is a m-times differntiable function that satisfies the initial value problem (Euler-Poisson-Darboux equation):
    \begin{align*}
        & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} U(x,r,t) - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U(x,r,t) - \frac{n-1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) =0, \quad (r,t) \in \mathbb{R}^+ \times \mathbb{R}^+ ,\\
        & U(x,r,0) = G(x,r), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} U(x,r,0) = H(x,r), \quad r \in \mathbb{R}^+.
    \end{align*}
}
\pf{
    We want to show that the equation for $u$ holds, if $u$ satisfies this initial value problem. Further, we need to show that all summands exist if $r \to 0$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item We begin with
    \begin{align*}
        U(x,r,t) &= \frac{1}{n \omega_n r^{n-1}} \int_{\partial B_r (x)} u(y,t) d \sigma (y) = \frac{1}{n \omega_n r^{n-1}} \int_{\partial B_1 (0)} u(x+r z,t) r^{n-1} d \sigma (z) \\
        &= \frac{1}{n \omega_n} \int_{\partial B_1 (0)} u(x+r z,t) d \sigma (z).
    \end{align*}
    where we see that the integral becomes independent of $t$ are $r$. It follows 
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\partial }{\partial r} U(x,r,t) &= \frac{1}{n \omega_n} \int_{\partial B_1 (0)} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} u(x+ry,t) d \sigma (y) = \frac{1}{n \omega_n} \int_{\partial B_1 (0)} \nabla_x u(x+ry,t) \cdot y d \sigma (y),\\
        &= \frac{1}{n \omega_n} \frac{r^n}{r^n} \int_{ B_1 (0)}  \nabla \cdot \nabla u (x+ry,t) d^n y = \frac{r^n}{n } \fint_{ B_1 (0)}  \nabla \cdot \nabla u (x+ry,t) d^n y \\
        &= \frac{r}{n } \fint_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y.
    \end{align*}
    The summand in the equation exists, due to 
    \[
    \lim_{r \to 0} \frac{n-1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) = \lim_{r \to 0} \frac{n-1}{n} \fint_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y = \frac{n-1}{n} \Delta u (x,t). 
    \]
    \item Next, we have 
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U(x,r,t) &= \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \frac{r}{n } \fint_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y\\
        &=  \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \frac{r}{ n \omega_n r^n } \int_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y = \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \frac{1}{ n \omega_n r^{n-1} } \int_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y\\
        &= \frac{-(n-1)}{ n \omega_n r^{n} } \int_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y + \frac{1}{ n \omega_n r^{n-1} } \int_{ B_r (x)} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \Delta u (y,t) d^n y \\
        &= \frac{1-n}{ n \omega_n r^{n} } \int_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y + \frac{1}{ n \omega_n r^{n-1} } \int_{ \partial B_r (x)} \Delta u (y,t) d \sigma(y) \\
        &= \left( \frac{1}{n} -1  \right) \fint_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y + \fint_{ \partial B_r (x)} \Delta u (y,t) d \sigma(y).
    \end{align*}
    And in the limit we have 
    \begin{align*}
    &\lim_{r \to 0 }  \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U(x,r,t) = \lim_{r \to 0 } \left( \left( \frac{1}{n} -1  \right) \fint_{ B_r (x)}  \Delta u (y,t) d^n y + \fint_{ \partial B_r (x)} \Delta u (y,t) d \sigma(y) \right) \\
    &= \left( \frac{1}{n} -1 \right) \Delta u (x,t) + \Delta u (x,t) = \frac{1}{n} \Delta u (x,t),
    \end{align*}
    which exists.
    \item Finally, due to the fact, that $u$ is the solution of the initial value problem stated on $\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+$ in the theorem, we have 
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\partial }{\partial r} r^{n-1} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) &= \frac{\partial }{\partial r} r^{n-1} \frac{r}{n} \fint_{B_r (x)} \Delta u(y,t) d^n y\\
        &=\frac{\partial }{\partial r} \frac{r^n}{n\omega_n r^n} \int_{B_r (x)} \Delta u(y,t) d^n y = \frac{\partial }{\partial r} \frac{1}{n\omega_n } \int_{B_r (x)} \Delta u(y,t) d^n y \\
        &=  \frac{1}{n\omega_n } \int_{\partial B_r (x)} \Delta u(y,t) d  \sigma(y ) \\
        &= \frac{1}{n\omega_n } \int_{\partial B_r (x)} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u(y,t) d  \sigma(y ) = r^{n-1}\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2}  \frac{1}{n\omega_n r^{n-1} } \int_{\partial B_r (x)}  u(y,t) d  \sigma(y )\\
        &= r^{n-1} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} U(x,r,t).
    \end{align*}
    This is equivalent to
    \begin{align*}
       & r^{n-1} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} U(x,r,t) = (n-1) r^{n-2} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) + r^{n-1} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U(x,r,t) \\
        &\iff \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} U(x,r,t) = \frac{n-1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) + \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U(x,r,t).
    \end{align*}
    \end{itemize}
     
  

}
\section{Solution in 3 Dimensions}
We will see that the wave equation can be transformed into a one dimensional wave equation, if the dimension is odd. However, if the dimension is even, this is not possible. We start with the three dimensional case $n=3$.\\
Fix $x \in \mathbb{R}^3$. Consider the initial value problem for the spherical mean
\begin{align*}
    & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} U(x,r,t) - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U(x,r,t) - \frac{2}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) =0, \quad (r,t) \in \mathbb{R}^+ \times \mathbb{R}^+ ,\\
    & U(x,r,0) = G(x,r), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} U(x,r,0) = H(x,r), \quad r \in \mathbb{R}^+.
\end{align*}
By substiuting $ \tilde{U} (x,r,t) := r U(x,r,t)$ we get
\begin{align*}
    & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \frac{\tilde{U}}{r} - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \frac{\tilde{U}}{r} - \frac{2}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \frac{\tilde{U}}{r} = \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{U} - \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \tilde{U} - \frac{\tilde{U}}{r^2} \right) - \frac{2}{r} \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \tilde{U} - \frac{\tilde{U}}{r^2} \right) \\
    &= \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{U} - \left( \frac{-1}{r^2} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \tilde{U} + \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \tilde{U} - \frac{1}{r^2} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \tilde{U} + \frac{2}{r^3} \tilde{U}  \right) + \frac{2}{r^3} \tilde{U} - \frac{2}{r^2} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \tilde{U} \\
    &= \frac{1}{r} \left( \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{U} - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \tilde{U} \right) =0\\
    &\iff  \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{U} - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \tilde{U}  =0
\end{align*}
Due to $\lim_{r \to 0} U(x,r,t) =u(x,t)$ it follows 
\[
\tilde{U} (x,0,t) = \lim_{r \to 0} r U(x,r,t,) =0 \cdot u(x,t)=0.
\]
We now get an additional condition for the transformed initial value problem:
\begin{align*}
    & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{U} - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \tilde{U} =0, \quad \text{ on } \{x\} \times \mathbb{R}^+ \times \mathbb{R}^+, \quad \tilde{U}(x,0,t) =0, \quad t \in \mathbb{R}_0^+ ,\\
    & \tilde{U}(x,r,0) = r G(x,r)=: \tilde{G}(x,r), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \tilde{U}(x,r,0) = r H(x,r) =: \tilde{H}(x,r), \quad r \in \mathbb{R}^+.
\end{align*}
This is the initial value problem of the last section whoose solution is given by 
\[
\tilde{U} (x,r,t) = \frac{1}{2} ( \tilde{G} (x,r+t) - \tilde{G} (x,t-r)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_{t-r}^{r+t} \tilde{H} (x,s) ds, \quad 0 \leq r \leq t.
\]
Note that we only consider the case $0\leq r \leq t$, because we are interested in $r \searrow 0$, due to the following: Using the continuity of $r \mapsto \tilde{U} (x,r,t)$ we get
\[
u(x,t) = \lim_{r \searrow 0} U(x,r,t) = \lim_{r \searrow 0} \frac{\tilde{U}(x,r,t)}{r}.
\]
Thus we can recover the solution for the 3D problem as the limit $r \searrow 0$: For all $x \in \mathbb{R}^3, t >0$:
\begin{align*}
    u(x,t) &= \lim_{r \searrow 0} \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{\tilde{G}(x,t+r)- \tilde{G}(x,t-r) + \tilde{G}(x,t) - \tilde{G}(x,t)}{r} \right) + \lim_{r \searrow 0} \frac{1}{2r} \int_{t-r}^{t+r} \tilde{H} (x,s) ds\\
    &= \lim_{r \searrow 0} \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{\tilde{G}(x,t+r) - \tilde{G}(x,t)}{r} -\frac{\tilde{G}(x,t-r)- \tilde{G}(x,t)}{r} \right) + \lim_{r \searrow 0} \frac{1}{2r} \int_{t-r}^{t+r} \tilde{H} (x,s) ds\\
    &= \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \tilde{G}(x,t) + \tilde{H}(x,t) \\
    &= \frac{\partial}{\partial t} (t G(x,t)) + t H(x,t) \\
    &= G(x,t) + t \frac{\partial}{\partial t} G(x,t) + t H(x,t) \\
    &= \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} g(y) d \sigma (y) + t \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} g(y) d \sigma (y) + t \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} h(y) d \sigma (y)\\
    &=  t \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \fint_{\partial B_1 (0)} g(x+tz) d \sigma (z) +  \fint_{\partial B_t (x)}(t h(y) + g(y)) d \sigma (y)\\
    &=  t  \fint_{\partial B_1 (0)} \nabla_x g(x+tz) \cdot z d \sigma (z) +  \fint_{\partial B_t (x)}(t h(y) + g(y)) d \sigma (y)\\
    &=  t  \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} \nabla_z g(z) \cdot \frac{z-x}{t} d \sigma (z) +  \fint_{\partial B_t (x)}(t h(y) + g(y)) d \sigma (y) \\
    &= \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} th(y) + g(y) + \nabla_y g(y) \cdot (y-x) d \sigma (y).
\end{align*}
Also called Kirchhoffs formula. Note that the solution at $(x,t)$ depends only on the values of $g$ and $h$ on the sphere $\partial B_t (x)$, i.e. information propagates with speed $1$ in all directions.

    
\section{Solution in 2 Dimensions}

In two dimensions the Euler-Poisson-Darboux equation cannot be transferred into the one dimensional wave equation. We present a method to transfer the two dimensional initial value problem into a three dimensional one, by choosing initial values that only depend on $x_1$ and $x_2$ but not on $x_3$.\\
Let $\bar{u}$ be on $\mathbb{R}^3 \times \mathbb{R}^+_0$ a solution of the initial value problem
\begin{align*}
    & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \bar{u} - \Delta \bar{u} =0, \quad \text{ on }\mathbb{R}^3 \times \mathbb{R}^+ ,\\
    & \bar{u}(x,0) = g(\bar{x}), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \bar{u}(x,0) = h(\bar{x}), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^3,
\end{align*} 
where $\bar{x} := (x_1,x_2) \in \mathbb{R}^2$. \\
Note that for a arbitary function $f$ that only depends on $\bar{x}$ it holds
\[
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_3} \fint_{\partial B_r (x)} f(y) d \sigma (y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x_3} \fint_{\partial B_r (0)} f(y+x) d \sigma (y) = \frac{1}{n \omega_n r^{n-1}} \int_{\partial B_r (0)}\underbrace{ \frac{\partial}{\partial x_3} f(y+x)}_{=0} d \sigma (y) =0,
\]
i.e. the mean over $\partial B_r (x)$ of $f$ is only dependent on $\bar{x}$ and $r$. \\
Because $\bar{u}$ is by definition independent of $x_3$ we have 
\[
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^3} \bar{u} = \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_1^2} \bar{u} + \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_2^2} \bar{u} +  \underbrace{\frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_3^2} \bar{u}}_{=0} = \Delta_{\mathbb{R}^2} \bar{u},
\]
Thus $\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \bar{u} - \Delta_{\mathbb{R}^3} \bar{u} =0 =\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \bar{u} - \Delta_{\mathbb{R}^2} \bar{u}$. Thus, if $u$ is the solution of the two dimensional initial value problem, then $\bar{u}(x,t)= u(\bar{x},t)$, $x \in \mathbb{R}^3$. In particular for $x:= (\bar{x},0)$ we have this equality. Using Kirchhoff's Formula it holds 
\[
\bar{u}(\underbrace{(\bar{x},0)}_{=x} ,t) = \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} th (y) + g(y) d \sigma (y) + \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} \nabla_{y} g(y) \cdot (y-x) d \sigma (y).
\]
We now want to transform this over to integrals on the two dimensional disc. For the Jacobi transformation, define the two parametrizations 
\begin{align*}
    & \phi_+ : B_r^2 (\bar{x}) \to \mathbb{R}^3, \quad z \mapsto (z,\gamma(z))^T := \begin{pmatrix}
        z_1 \\ z_2 \\ \sqrt{r^2 -|z-\bar{x}|^2}
    \end{pmatrix} \\
    & \phi_- : B_r^2 (\bar{x}) \to \mathbb{R}^3, \quad z \mapsto (z,-\gamma(z))^T := \begin{pmatrix}
        z_1 \\ z_2 \\ -\sqrt{r^2 -|z-\bar{x}|^2}
    \end{pmatrix} .
\end{align*}
The equator $\{ y \in B_r^3 (x) \mid y_3 =0 \}$ has measure zero and can thus be neglected. The Jacobi Matrix is 
\[
D \phi_+ = \begin{pmatrix}
    1 & 0\\
    0 & 1 \\
   - \frac{1}{2} 2 (z_1-x_1) (r^2- |z-\bar{x}|^2)^{-1/2} & - \frac{1}{2} 2 (z_2-x_2) (r^2- |z-\bar{x}|^2)^{-1/2}
\end{pmatrix}
\]
and 
\[
D \phi_- = \begin{pmatrix}
    1 & 0\\
    0 & 1 \\
    \frac{1}{2} 2 (z_1-x_1) (r^2- |z-\bar{x}|^2)^{-1/2} &  \frac{1}{2} 2 (z_2-x_2) (r^2- |z-\bar{x}|^2)^{-1/2}
\end{pmatrix}
\]
then 
\[
(D \phi_+)^T D \phi = \begin{pmatrix}
    1+ (z_1 -x_1)^2 (r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2)^{-1} & (z_1 -x_1) (z_2-x_2) (r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2)^{-1}  \\
    (z_1 -x_1) (z_2-x_2) (r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2)^{-1}  & 1+ (z_2 -x_2)^2 (r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2)^{-1}
\end{pmatrix} = (D \phi_-)^T D \phi_-,
\]
which leads to 
\begin{align*}
\det ( (D \phi_+)^T D \phi  ) &= \left( 1 + \frac{(z_1 - x_1)^2}{r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2} \right) \left( 1 + \frac{(z_2 - x_2)^2}{r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2} \right) - \left(  \frac{(z_1 - x_1)^2 (z_2 - x_2)^2}{(r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2)^2} \right) \\
&=1+  \frac{(z_1 - x_1)^2+ (z_2 - x_2)^2}{r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2} = \frac{r^2}{r^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2} .
\end{align*}
This now implies that for any $f$ 
\begin{align*}
&\fint_{\partial B_t^3 (x)} f(z) d \sigma (z)=\fint_{\partial B_t^3 (x) \{y >0 \}} f(z) d \sigma (z) + \fint_{\partial B_t^3 (x) \{y <0 \}} f(z) d \sigma (z) \\
&= \frac{1}{4 \pi t^2} \int_{ B_t^2 (x) } f(z) \sqrt{\det ( (D \phi_+)^T D\phi_+ )} d^2 z + \frac{1}{4 \pi t^2} \int_{ B_t^2 (x)} f(z) \sqrt{\det ( (D \phi_-)^T D\phi_- )} d^2 z \\
&= 2 \frac{1}{4 \pi t^2} \int_{ B_t^2 (x)} f(z) \frac{t}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z- \bar{x}|^2}} d^2 z = \frac{1}{2 \pi t^2} \int_{ B_t^2 (x)} f(z) \frac{t}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z- \bar{x}|^2}} d^2 z \\
&= \frac{1}{2 \pi t} \int_{ B_t^2 (x)} f(z) \frac{1}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z- \bar{x}|^2}} d^2 z = \frac{t}{2}  \fint_{ B_t^2 (x)} f(z) \frac{1}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z- \bar{x}|^2}} d^2 z
\end{align*}
Applying this now to the Kirchhoff's Formula we get for $x := (\bar{x},0)$
\begin{align*}
    u(\bar{x},t) &= \bar{u} (x,t) = \fint_{\partial B_t^3 (x)} th (y) + g(y) d \sigma (y) + \fint_{\partial B_t^3 (x)} \nabla_{y} g(y) \cdot (y-x) d \sigma (y) \\
    &= \frac{t}{2} \fint_{B_t^2 (\bar{x})} \frac{th(z) + g(z)}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2}} d^2 z +  \frac{t}{2} \fint_{B_t^2 (\bar{x})} \frac{\nabla_z g(z) \cdot (z- \bar{x})}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2}} d^2 z \\
    &= \frac{t}{2} \fint_{B_t^2 (\bar{x})} \frac{th(z) + g(z)+\nabla_z g(z) \cdot (z- \bar{x})}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z-\bar{x}|^2}} d^2 z 
\end{align*}
In three dimensions we had that the solution only depended on $\partial B_t (x)$. This implied that information triversion speed is $1$. In the two dimensional case we now have the solution depending on the entire ball $B_t (\bar{x})$, which yields that the information speed is everything from $0$ to $1$.\\

Further, we call the method of deriving the solution of an initial value problem, by transforming the problem onto an initial value problem in a higher dimensional space, method of descent. In that higher dimensional space the initial values do not depend on all coordinates. We need to show that the solution also deos not depend on these coordinates. We just did this in two dimensions, i.e. $\bar{u}(x,t) = u(\bar{x},t)$.\\
A natural question is weather we can apply this method to the solution of the initial value problem in one dimension.

\section{Solution in odd Dimensions}
In any odd dimension we can transform the Euler-Poisson-Darboux equation into the one dimensional wave equation.
\lem{}{
    Let $\phi: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ be a $k+1$ times continuously differentiable function, then $\forall k \in \mathbb{N}$ it holds 
    \begin{align*}
        &i) \; \left( \frac{d}{dr} \right)^2 \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{d}{dr} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} \phi (r) = \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{d}{dr} \right)^k r^{2k} \frac{d}{dr} \phi (r) \\
        &ii) \; \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{d}{dr} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} \phi (r) = \sum_{j=0}^{k-1} \beta_{k,j} r^{j+1} \frac{d^j}{d r^j} \phi (r), \quad \text{where $\beta_{k,j}$ do not depend on }\phi \\
        &iii) \; \beta_{k,0} = 1 \cdot 3 \cdot 5 \cdot ... \cdot (2k-1) = \frac{(2k-1)!}{2 \cdot 4 \cdot ... \cdot (2k-2)} = \frac{(2k-1)!}{2^{k-1} (k-1)!}
    \end{align*}
}
\pf{
    Skipped 
}
Let the dimension be $n= 2k+1 \geq 3$ and $u \in C^{k+1} (\mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}_0^+)$ be the solution of the initial value problem 
\begin{align*}
    & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u =0 ,\quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+ \\
    &U(x,0)=g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0)=h(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n.
\end{align*}
Define 
\begin{align*}
    & \tilde{U}(x,r,t) := \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} U(x,r,t) \\
    & \tilde{G}(x,t) := \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} G(x,r), \quad \tilde{H}(x,r) := \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} H(x,r).
\end{align*}
\lem{}{
    If $u \in C^{k+1} (\mathbb{R}^{2k+1} \times \mathbb{R}_0^+)$ solves the initial value problem of the wave equation, then $\tilde{U}(x,r,t)$ solves for all fixed $x \in \mathbb{R}^{2k+1}$ the following initial value problem 
    \begin{align*}
        &\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{U} - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \tilde{U} = 0, \quad \text{ on } \{x\} \times \mathbb{R}^+ \times \mathbb{R}^+, \quad \tilde{U}(x,0,t)=0, \quad t \in \mathbb{R}_0^+\\
        & \tilde{U}(x,r,0)=\tilde{G}(x,r) , \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \tilde{U} (x,r,0) = \tilde{H}(x,r), \quad r \in \mathbb{R}^+.
    \end{align*}
}
\pf{
    Let $U$ be the solution of the Euler-Poisson-Darboux equation in dimension $n=2k+1$, then we have 
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \tilde{U} (x,r,t) & \stackrel{Def.}{=} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} U(x,r,t)\\
        &\stackrel{i)}{=} \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^k r^{2k} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) \\
        &= \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{k-1} \left( \frac{2k r^{2k-1}}{r}  \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) + \frac{1}{r} r^{2k} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U(x,r,t) \right) \\
         &= \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} \left( \frac{2k}{r}  \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U(x,r,t) +  \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U(x,r,t) \right) \\
         &\stackrel{(*)}{=} \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} U(x,r,t) = \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{U}(x,r,t),
    \end{align*}
    altough we used in $(*)$ that $2k=n-1$ by definition of the dimension. Finally, we need to check the boundary condition,i.e. to show that $\tilde{U}$ vanishes as $r\to 0$: 
    \begin{align*}
        \lim_{r \to 0} \tilde{U}(x,r,t) &= \lim_{r \to 0} \left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{k-1} r^{2k-1} U(x,r,t) \stackrel{ii)}{=} \lim_{r \to 0}  \sum_{j=0}^{k-1} \beta_{k,j} r^{j+1} \frac{\partial^j}{\partial r^j} U(x,r,t) \\
        &=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1} \beta_{k,j} \cdot 0 \cdot \underbrace{ \frac{\partial^j}{\partial r^j} U(x,r,t) \big|_{r=0}}_{< \infty}=0.
    \end{align*}
}
Thus, we have reduced the odd dimensional initial value problem into a one dimensional initial value problem on the intervall $[0,\infty)$. As we have already considered this problem, we know its solution 
\[
\tilde{U} (x,r,t) = \frac{1}{2} (\tilde{G}(x,t+r)- \tilde{G}(x,t-r)) + \frac{1}{2} \int_{t-r}^{t+r} \tilde{H}(x,s) ds.
\]
With the relation
\begin{align*}
&\tilde{U}(x,r,t) = \sum_{j=0}^{k-1} \beta_{k,j} r^{j+1} \frac{\partial^j}{\partial r^j} U(x,r,t) = \beta_{k,0} r U(x,r,t) + \mathcal{O}(r^2) \\
&\iff \frac{\tilde{U}(x,r,t)}{\beta_{k,0} r} = U(x,r,t) + \mathcal{O}(r) \\
& \iff \lim_{r \to 0} \frac{\tilde{U}(x,r,t)}{\beta_{k,0} r} = \lim_{r \to 0} U(x,r,t) = u(x,t),
\end{align*}
which finally implies that 
\begin{align*}
    u(x,t) &= \frac{1}{\beta_{k,0}} \lim_{r \to 0} \frac{\tilde{U}(x,r,t)}{r} = \frac{2^{k-1} (k-1)!}{(2k-1)!} \lim_{r \to 0}\left(\frac{1}{2r} (\tilde{G}(x,t+r)- \tilde{G}(x,t-r)) + \frac{1}{2r} \int_{t-r}^{t+r} \tilde{H}(x,s) ds \right) \\
    &= \frac{2^{k-1} (k-1)!}{(2k-1)!} \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \tilde{G}(x,t) + \tilde{H}(x,t) \right).
\end{align*}
\thm{}{
    For odd $n\geq 3$ the solution of the initial value problem 
    \[
    \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u =0, \quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+, \quad u(x,0)=g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0)=h(x), x \in \mathbb{R}^n,
    \]
    with $g \in C^{\frac{n+3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^n)$, $h \in C^{\frac{n+1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ the solution is given by 
    \[
    u(x,t) = \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})! }{(n-2)!} \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-3}{2}} t^{n-2} \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} g(y) d \sigma (y) + \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-3}{2}} t^{n-2} \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} h(y) d \sigma (y) \right).
    \]
}
\pf{We can split the above initial value problem into the following subproblems 
\[
\begin{cases}
    \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u =0, \quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+\\
    u(x,0)=0, \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\\
    \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0)=h(x), x \in \mathbb{R}^n,
\end{cases} \quad \text{ and }\quad \begin{cases}
    \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u =0, \quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+\\
    u(x,0)=h(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n,\\
    \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0)=0, x \in \mathbb{R}^n,
\end{cases}
\]
I.e. if $u$ and $v$ are the solutions to the first and second initial value problem respectively, then their sum $u+v$ is the solution to the initial value problem in the theorem.\\
Note that if $v$ is the solution to the first problem, then $u(x,t):=\frac{\partial}{\partial t} v(x,t) $ is the solution to the second problem: 
\begin{align*}
   & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u = \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \left( \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} v - \Delta v \right) =0 \\
    & u(x,0) = \frac{\partial}{\partial t} v(x,0) = h(x) \\
    & \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) = \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} v(x,0) = \Delta v(x,0)=0.
\end{align*}
Thus, we only need to focus on the first initial value problem. Using that $n-1=2k \iff k-1= \frac{n-3}{2}$, we get with $g=0$: 
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u(x,t) &= \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \frac{2^{k-1} (k-1)!}{(2k-1)!} \tilde{H}(x,t)  = \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \frac{2^{k-1} (k-1)!}{(2k-1)!} \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{k-1} t^{2k-1} H(x,t) \\
        &\stackrel{i)}{=}  \frac{2^{k-1} (k-1)!}{(2k-1)!} \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{k} t^{2k} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} H(x,t) \\
        &=  \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}   t^{n-1} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \frac{1}{n \omega_n t^{n-1}} \int_{\partial B_t (x)} h(y) d\sigma (y) \\
        &=  \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}   t^{n-1} \frac{\partial}{\partial t}\frac{1}{n \omega_n t^{n-1}} \int_{\partial B_1 (0)} h(x+tz) t^{n-1} d\sigma (z) \\
        &=  \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}   t^{n-1} \frac{1}{n \omega_n } \int_{\partial B_1 (0)} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} h(x+tz)  d\sigma (z) \\
        &=  \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}   t^{n-1} \frac{1}{n \omega_n t^{n-1} } \int_{\partial B_1 (0)} \nabla_x t^{n-1} h(x+tz) \cdot z  d\sigma (z) \\
        &=  \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}   t^{n-1} \frac{1}{n \omega_n t^{n-1} } \int_{\partial B_t (x)}  \nabla h(z) \cdot \underbrace{\frac{z-x}{t}}_{=N(z)}  d\sigma (z) \\
        &= \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}} \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right) \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{-1}  \frac{1}{n \omega_n  } \int_{ B_t (x)} \Delta h(z) d^n z \\
         &= \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-3}{2}}   \frac{1}{n \omega_n t } \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \int_{ B_t (x)} \Delta h(z) d^n z\\
         &= \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-3}{2}}   \frac{1}{n \omega_n t }  \int_{ \partial B_t (x)} \Delta h(z) d^n z\\
        &= \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-3}{2}} t^{n-2}  \frac{1}{n \omega_n t^{n-1} }  \int_{ \partial B_t (0)} \Delta h(z+x) d^n z\\
        &= \frac{2^{\frac{n-3}{2}} (\frac{n-3}{2})!}{(n-2)!} t \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-3}{2}} t^{n-2}   \fint_{ \partial B_t (0)} \Delta h(z+x) d^n z = \Delta u(x,t).
    \end{align*}
    Finally, it only remains to show the boundary limit for $t \to 0$. First, notice that 
    \[
    \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-3}{2}} t^{n-2} = \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}} (n-2) t^{n-4}  \sim t^{n-2- 2 \frac{n-3}{2}} = t^1.
    \]
    Using this we get that the solution is asymptotically equvalent wrt. $t$ to :
    \[
    u(x,t) \sim \frac{\partial}{\partial t} t \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} g(y) d \sigma (y) + t \fint_{\partial B_t(x)} h(y) d \sigma (y).
    \]
    Thus we get 
    \begin{align*}
     & u(x,0)=  \lim_{t \to 0} \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} t \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} g(y) d \sigma (y) + t \fint_{\partial B_t(x)} h(y) d \sigma (y) \right) = g(x) \\
      &\frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} t \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} g(y) d \sigma (y) + t \fint_{\partial B_t(x)} h(y) d \sigma (y) \right) \\
      &= \lim_{t \to 0}  \left( \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} t \fint_{\partial B_t (x)} g(y) d \sigma (y) +\frac{\partial}{\partial t} t \fint_{\partial B_t(x)} h(y) d \sigma (y) \right) \\
      &=0+ \lim_{t \to 0}  \left(  t \fint_{\partial B_1 (x)} \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} g(ty) d \sigma (y) +\frac{\partial}{\partial t} t \fint_{\partial B_t(x)} h(y) d \sigma (y) \right) \\
      &=  \lim_{t \to 0}  \left(  t \fint_{\partial B_t (x)}\Delta g(y) d \sigma (y) +\frac{\partial}{\partial t} t \fint_{\partial B_t(x)} h(y) d \sigma (y) \right) =h(x).
    \end{align*}
}
It is visible that the solution only depends on the boundary $\partial B_t (x)$, i.e.  the speed of propagation is 1. Additionally, $u$ is at $(x,t)$ $\frac{n-1}{2}$ times less differentiable than $g$ and $\frac{n-3}{2}$ less differentiable than $h$.\\
In contrast the heat equation has infinite speed and regardless of the regularity of the initial value, the solution is for some $t>0$ smooth.

\section{Solution in even Dimension}
Again, we use the method of descent meaning we formulate the $n$ dimensional problem into a $n+1$ dimensional problem with initial values $g$ and $h$ on $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ not depending on $x_{n+1}$. This immediatly implies that 
\[
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n+1}} g(x) =0, \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n+1}} h(x) =0.
\]
Their partial derivates of the integrals on the boundary vanish as well as a result of that: 
\[
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n+1}} \fint_{\partial B_t(x)} g(y) d\sigma (y) = \fint_{ B_t(0)} \frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n+1}} g(x+y) d\sigma (y) = 0.
\]
Because the above holds for all $t>0$ it follows that 
\[
\frac{\partial}{\partial t} \frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n+1}} \fint_{\partial B_t(x)} g(y) d\sigma (y)
\]
Let $u$ be the solution of the $2k$ dimensional problem and $v$ the solution to the $2k+1$ dimensional problem, then with the mapping $i: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^{n+1}, x \mapsto (x,0)$ we have 
\[
u(x,t) \stackrel{independence}{=} (v \circ i) (x,t) = v(i(x),t) = v((x,0),t), 
\]
i.e. we can obtain the solution in $2k$ dimensions by solving the $2k+1$ problem and evaluating it at $x_{n+1}=0$. Similar to before, define the parametrization 
\begin{align*}
     & \phi_+ : B_r^n (\bar{x}) \to \mathbb{R}^{n+1}, \quad z \mapsto (z,\gamma(z))^T := \begin{pmatrix}
        z_1 \\ \hdots\\ z_n \\ \sqrt{r^2 -|z-\bar{x}|^2}
    \end{pmatrix} \\
    & \phi_- : B_r^n (\bar{x}) \to \mathbb{R}^{n+1}, \quad z \mapsto (z,-\gamma(z))^T := \begin{pmatrix}
        z_1 \\ \hdots \\ z_n \\ -\sqrt{r^2 -|z-\bar{x}|^2}
    \end{pmatrix} .
\end{align*}
Its Jacobian is then 
\[
D\phi_+ = \begin{pmatrix}
  &  Id_{n\times n}& \\
  (x_1 - z_1) \gamma (z)^{-1} & \cdot & (x_n - z_n) \gamma (z)^{-1} 
\end{pmatrix}
\]
and $\det ( (D\phi_+)^T D\phi_+ ) = 1 + \gamma (z)^{-2} \sum_{i=1}^n (z_i -x_i)^2= \frac{r^2}{\gamma (z)^2} $.\\
For $\bar{f}: \mathbb{R}^{n+1} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $f:= \bar{f} \circ \phi_\pm: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}$ we have 
\begin{align*}
   & \fint_{\partial B_t^{n+1} (x)} \bar{f} (y) d \sigma (y) = \frac{1}{(n+1) \omega_{n+1} t^n} \int_{\partial B_t^{n+1}(x)} \bar{f}(y) d \sigma (y) \\
    &=  \frac{1}{(n+1) \omega_{n+1} t^n}\left( \int_{ B_t^{n}(x) \cap \{ y_{n+1}>0 \} } f(y) \sqrt{\det (D\phi_+)^T D \phi_+)} d^n y + \int_{ B_t^{n}(x) \cap \{ y_{n+1}<0 \} } f(y) \sqrt{\det (D\phi_-)^T D \phi_-)} d^n y \right)\\
    &=  \frac{1}{(n+1) \omega_{n+1} t^n}\left( \int_{ B_t^{n}(x)  } f(y) \sqrt{\det (D\phi_+)^T D \phi_+)} d^n y + \int_{ B_t^{n}(x)  } f(y) \sqrt{\det (D\phi_-)^T D \phi_-)} d^n y \right)\\
    &=  \frac{2}{(n+1) \omega_{n+1} t^n}\int_{ B_t^{n}(x)  } f(y)\frac{t}{\gamma (y)}  d^n y \\
    &=  \frac{2\omega_n t^n}{(n+1) \omega_{n+1} t^n}\fint_{ B_t^{n}(x)  } f(y)\frac{t}{\gamma (y)}  d^n y \\
    &=  \frac{2\omega_n t}{(n+1) \omega_{n+1}}\fint_{ B_t^{n}(x)  } f(y)\frac{1}{\sqrt{t^2- |y-x|^2}}  d^n y \\
\end{align*}

\thm{}{
    Let $n$ be positive and even, $g \in C^{\frac{n+4}{2}} (\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $h\in C^{\frac{n+2}{2}} (\mathbb{R}^n)$, then the solution ot the initial value problem 
    \begin{align*}
       & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u =0, \quad \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+,\\
        & u(x,0) = g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) = h(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n,
    \end{align*}
    is given at $(x,t) \in \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+_0$ by 
    \[
    u(x,t) = \frac{1}{2^{n/2} \frac{n}{2} ! } \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-2}{2}} t^n \fint_{B_t (x)} \frac{g(z)}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z-x|^2}} d^n z + \left( \frac{1}{t} \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \right)^{\frac{n-2}{2}} t^n  \fint_{B_t (x)} \frac{h(z)}{\sqrt{t^2 - |z-x|^2}} d^n z \right).
    \]
}
\pf{
    Unclear ???
}

\section{Inhomogeneous Wave Equation}
We interpret in the inhomogeneous wave equation $f$ as an external force:
\begin{align*}
   &\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u =f, \quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+\\
   &u(x,0)=0, \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) =0, \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n.
\end{align*}
We can interpret the solution to the inhomogeneous wave eqation as the integral of the solution to the homogeneous wave eqaution with the external force reflected in the initial condition: 
\begin{align*}
    & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} v_s- \Delta v_s =0, \quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times [s, \infty)\\
    &v_s(x,s)=0, \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} v_s(x,s) =f(x,s), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n
\end{align*}
and $u(x,t) = \int_0^t v_s (x,t) ds$. This true because with the Leipniz rule:
\begin{align*}
    &\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u(x,t) = \frac{\partial}{\partial t} \left( \underbrace{v_t(x,t)}_{=0} + \int_0^t \frac{\partial}{\partial t} v_s (x,t) ds \right) = \frac{\partial}{\partial t} v_t(x,t) + \int_0^t \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} v_s (x,t) ds \\
    &= f(x,t) + \int_0^t \Delta v_s (x,t) = f(x,t) + \Delta u(x,t).
\end{align*}
The sum of the solutions to the homoegenous and inhmomogenous wave equation solve by linearity of the differential operator the following initial value problem 
\begin{align*}
      &\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u =f, \quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+\\
   &u(x,0)=g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,0) =h(x), \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^n.
\end{align*}
When determining the past from the present, define $v(x,\tau) := u(x,-t)$, where $\tau = - t$. Then 
\[
\frac{\partial}{\partial \tau} v(x,\tau) = - \frac{\partial}{\partial t} v(x,\tau), \quad \frac{\partial^2}{\partial \tau^2} v(x,\tau) =  \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} v(x,\tau).
\]
This means that the PDE does not change, but the initial velocity does: 
\begin{align*}
    &\frac{\partial^2}{\partial \tau^2} v - \Delta v= f(x,-\tau) \quad \text{ on } \mathbb{R}^n \times \mathbb{R}^+\\
    & v(x,0) = g(x), \quad \frac{\partial}{\partial \tau} v(x,0) = -h(x).
\end{align*}

\section{Energy Methods}
Hyperbolic PDEs do not satisfy maximum principle???\\
The theorem for the maximum principle for elliptic second order PDEs applies to degenerate elliptic PDEs???
\thm{}{
    Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^n$ be a bounded domain, then the initial value problem 
    \begin{align*}
        & \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u - \Delta u =f, \quad \text{ on } \Omega \times (0,T) \\
        &u(x,t) = g(x,t), \quad \text{ on $\Omega \times \{0\}$ and $\partial \Omega \times (0,T)$ }\\
        & \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,t) = h(x), \quad \text{ on } \Omega \times \{ 0 \},
    \end{align*}
    has a unique solution in $C^2(\Omega \times (0,T))$ and 
    \[
    \forall |\alpha | \leq 2: \quad \partial^\alpha u \text{ extends continuously to } \bar{\Omega} \times [0,T].
    \]
}
\pf{
    Let $u$ be the solution of the initial value problem with $f=g=h=0$ and define 
    \[
    e(t) := \frac{1}{2} \int_\Omega \left( \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,t) \right)^2 + \left( \nabla u(x,t) \right)^2 d^n x.
    \]
    Then it follows 
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d}{dt} e(t) &= \int_\Omega \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u(x,t) \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,t) + \underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial t} \nabla u(x,t) \nabla u(x,t)}_{= \nabla \partial_t u \nabla u = (\partial_t u \nabla u )^\prime - \partial_t u \Delta u} d^n x \\
        &= \int_\Omega \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u(x,t) \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,t) + \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u (x,t) \Delta u(x,t) d^n x \\
        &= \int_\Omega \frac{\partial}{\partial t} u(x,t) \left(  \frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} u(x,t)  + \Delta u(x,t) \right) d^n x=0.
    \end{align*}
    Because we set $e(0)=0$ and there is no change, it stays zero for all $t >0 $.
}





\section{Summary}
We begin with the one dimensional case, wehre the differential operator factorizes itno the two transport operators $(\frac{\partial}{\partial t} - \frac{\partial}{\partial x}) (\frac{\partial}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial}{\partial x})$. This implies that the solution is the sum of a wave moving to the right and one to the left, $F(x+t)+G(x-t)$. Adding n initial position and velocity to the problem yields the D'Alembert's formula. It shows the domain of dependence is $[x-t,x+t]$ confirming finite speed of propagation. We also extend this method to the half-line problem $\mathbb{R}^+$ using the method of reflection and extending $u,g,h$ to odd functions.\\
Defining the wave eqaution in terms of spherical means establishes the Euler-Poisson-Darboux equation 
\[
\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} U - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} U - \frac{n-1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} U =0,
\]
whcih transforms the PDE into a one dimensional one. In $n=3$ substuting $\tilde{U} := r U$ transforms the Euler-Poisson-Darboux equation the one dimensional wave equation $\frac{\partial^2}{\partial t^2} \tilde{U} - \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r^2} \tilde{U} =0$. Solving this using D'Alembert's formula and taking the limit $r \to 0$ yields Kirchhoffs formula. In this case $(n=3)$ the solution only depends on the sphere $\partial B_t (x)$, which means that in $3D$ propagation is on sharp wavefronts without a "wake". \\
For $n=2$ we use the method of descent, meaning we formulate the $2D$ problem as a special case of the $3D$ problem, by setting the initial data nad solution such that is is independend from the third coordinate $x_3$. Using Kirchhoffs formula in $3D$ and transforming the integrals to $2D$ yields a formolation for the solution. However, it depends on the entire ball $B_t (x)$, i.e. propagation occurs including wakes.\\
Generalizing for odd dimensions, multipliying the spherical means by $\left( \frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial}{\partial r} \right)^{n-1} r^{2k-1}$ a specific form is derived for the solution.\\
Generalizing fir even dimensions, we do the same appraoch by setting initial data nad solution such taht it is independend from the $n+1^{th}$ coordinate, giving a formula for the solution. \\
